(5) NEGATIVE KEPT: hdr[0] (1,432 for 622, just past the largest id) is a candidate for the cumulative
object count after the blob, but it does NOT hold on entry 621 (hdr[0] 32,196 < hdr[1]+hdr[5] 39,257),
so it is something else.

METHOD NOTE: a few anchors are wrong because several count-1 lists hold the same authored position
(GENE-19/29 matched a duplicate); those give B far outside the monotone run and are dropped. (measured on five packs, scripts/igb_sections.py). Section 1 (hdr[1]) is
the **memory/object directory** -- igMemoryDirEntry + igObjectDirEntry -- and section 2 (hdr[5]) holds
the pack's game objects. Counts:
 entry 622: dir 50 (36 igMemoryDirEntry + 14 igObjectDirEntry) | objects 1206
 entry 183: dir 33 (19 + 14) | objects 266
 entry 621: dir 1122 (813 + 309) | objects 38135
 entry 140: dir 6 (2 + 4) | objects 5
 entry 179: dir 891 (627 + 264) | objects 28933
So the directory is a real object chain too, and its entries (the arena blocks and the objects they
hold) are part of the same id space -- a candidate source of the per-record extras, though the counts
alone do not fit a simple "one extra per directory entry" model (36 entries for entry 622 against 21
extras by record 895).

(7) LEAD FOR THE PER-RECORD COUNT (evidence, not yet confirmed): a flat sequential read order is hard
to reconcile with the smallest refs. Entry 622's first section-2 records are 50 beGeneraterDataList,
51 beBaseInfoRamList, 52 beGeneraterInfo, and record 52 already carries object refs 4 and 5. With the
directory + these records ahead of it, a flat order makes record 52's own id ~52 (or ~2 if the
directory is not numbered), so refs 4/5 would be forward references -- but FUN_00305580 resolves a ref
by looking up an object that must already exist. So the reader very likely builds the list in
**dependency order** (a referenced record is read/created before its referrer), i.e. the file's record
order is storage order, not creation order, and id(t) is the position of record t in that traversal.
That is exactly the shape the B curve shows: deterministic (the graph is fixed) but not a function of
the record's class alone (it depends on the reference graph). Replicating the traversal from the loader
code, or solving the id(t) assignment as a post-order DFS over the file's reference graph, is the next
step.

**Evidence:** scripts/igb_sections.py; docs/engine/igbinary-format.md; NDMW.AFS entry 622

<a id="f460"></a>
### #460 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x002e7640 — FUN_002e7640 builds the global object list from the table; FUN_002d7e90(ctx, id) is the lookup; scripts/igb_ids.py

T14 SOLVED: an igObjectRef id indexes a pack-local remap table that sits between the pack's two object sections -- header {u32 tableBytes}{u32 idCount} + idCount u16. Object entries (igObjectDirEntry) are in the same order as section-2's records; memory entries (igMemoryDirEntry) are the objects' memory blocks. id = table position; the space is pack-local and needs no load replay.

**Notes:** T14 SOLVED -- THE igObjectRef ID IS AN INDEX INTO A PACK-LOCAL REMAP TABLE, IN THE FILE.

WHERE IT IS. Between the pack's two object sections (section 1 = the memory/object
directory, section 2 = the game objects) sits a table:

 {u32 tableBytes}{u32 idCount} followed by idCount u16 (tableBytes == 8 + 2*idCount)

measured on five NDMW.AFS packs, and its length matches the largest id the pack uses:
 entry 622: @+0x1580 1434 ids = 1206 objects + 228 memory blocks
 entry 183: @+0x1376 328 ids = 266 objects + 62
 entry 621: @+0xc2dc 50497 ids = 38135 objects + 12362
 entry 179: @+0xa482 38665 ids = 28933 objects + 9732
 entry 140: @+0x0c1c 7 ids = 5 objects + 2

WHAT IT MEANS. Each u16 indexes SECTION 1 (the directory). Entries that land on an
**igObjectDirEntry** enumerate the pack's objects, and they are in **exactly the same
order as section 2's records** -- verified class-for-class on all five packs (622:
1206/1206, 621: 38135/38135, 179: 28933/28933). Entries that land on an
**igMemoryDirEntry** are the objects' memory blocks. The table position, memory entries
included, is the global id a field stores, so

 id -> (the k-th object entry in the table) -> section-2 record k

and every reference in a pack resolves inside that pack's own table. No load replay.

VERIFIED END TO END. Every igObjectRef in the pack resolves in range, and every field
maps to exactly one target class:
 entry 622: 304 refs, 0 out of range; beGeneraterData._genePosList -> igVec3fList 72/72,
 _geneRotYRadianList -> igFloatList 72/72, _playerDataList ->
 beGeneraterPlayerDataList 72/72, beGeneraterInfo._dataList ->
 beGeneraterDataList 1/1, beGeneraterItemData._dataList ->
 beGeneraterItemDataOneList 34/34, beGeneraterPlayerData._itemDataList and
 _itemLastDataList -> beGeneraterItemDataList 26/26 each
 entry 183: 67 refs, 0 out of range, same one-class-per-field result
 entry 621: 26699 refs, 0 out of range, one class per field (e.g. ParticleTimer.slot7
 -> igNodeList 161, beAction2Data._comList -> igNamedObjectList 739)
 entry 140: 1 ref (beFileListInfo._fnames -> igNamedObjectList)
Spot check against the running game: GENE-00 (beGeneraterData record 846, `_pos`
(-117,72,609)) has `_genePosList` = 916, and id 916 resolves to record 845, an
igVec3fList whose pool content is exactly (-117,72,609) -- the same cell-for-cell match
#420 got live, now read with no emulator.

WHAT THIS CORRECTS. #426 concluded the ids were a load-wide creation ordinal that could
not be resolved from one pack, because entry 622's ids reached ~1430 while its section
held 1206 records. The 228-entry gap is not earlier packs: it is the pack's own
**memory blocks** (the `_data` blocks of its list records), and the id space is the
pack's table (0-based, 1434 entries). That also explains everything #421 measured: the
"extras" that drift with position are the memory blocks interleaved in the table, which
is why `id - recordIndex` is deterministic but not per-class -- the table's OBJ order
is the record order and the MEM entries are its inserts; `id = recordIndex +
(memory blocks before it in the table)`. No load-level replay, no manifest and no
section list are needed.

CAVEAT / SCOPE. The table has been validated on these five packs; the big packs needed
the full record walk (38135/28933 records) to confirm, which the tool does. Cross-pack
references would appear as ids outside the table and did not occur in any of the five.

TOOL: scripts/igb_ids.py FILE --blob OFF [--id N] [--check-refs]. Topic head for igObjectRef id binding via pack-local remap table (T14): also read #421, #426 (folded in, kept for their evidence).

**Evidence:** scripts/igb_ids.py; docs/engine/igbinary-format.md

Worked example on entry 622 (stag0102's generator pack), walking
beGeneraterData._playerDataList -> beGeneraterPlayerDataList -> beGeneraterPlayerData._itemDataList
-> beGeneraterItemDataList -> beGeneraterItemData -> _dataList -> beGeneraterItemDataOneList

<a id="f462"></a>
### #462 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x21307000 — NDMW.AFS entries 622/623/624; scripts/igb_generators.py; D1 tier variants (#429), D2 difficulty split

The three stag0102 tier packs (622/623/624) are difficulty bands that differ ONLY in beGeneraterData._geneEnemyId: same 72 generators, positions, timers, modes and the same 772-row drop table; the enemy id shifts by a per-generator constant (-4,+4)x37, (-3,+3)x16, (-1,+1)x15, (0,0)x3, (-2,+2)x1, selecting consecutive stat-row bands.

**Notes:** THE THREE stag0102 TIER PACKS DIFFER ONLY IN _geneEnemyId. Entry 622/623/624 were known to be
"row-tier variants" (#429) but not measured. With the remap table (#460) all three decode offline
(scripts/igb_generators.py) and are IDENTICAL except for one field:

 * same 72 generators, same names, same positions, same startData timers, same startMode/fillMode,
 same callLua, and the SAME 772-row drop table (set-identical, 0 rows differ);
 * 0 of 72 generators differ in pos/timer/mode between 622 and 623;
 * _geneEnemyId differs for 69 of 72, by a per-generator constant:
 (-4, +4) 37 generators (-3, +3) 16 (-1, +1) 15 (0, 0) 3 (-2, +2) 1
 i.e. 623 = 622 shifted down a band and 624 = 622 shifted up, so the three packs select three
 consecutive STAT-ROW bands in the 649-row enemy table (D4). Worked example GENE-00: enemy 36
 (622) / 33 (623) / 39 (624) at the same position (-117, 72, 609).
 * the roll group is the same shift: 622 has 380/383/386/389/392, 623 has 379/382/385/388/391,
 624 has 381/384/387/390/393 -- the GENE-XX crown/elite variants of finding #190.

CONSEQUENCE for D2: the drop tables are NOT difficulty-split -- they are identical across the three
tiers. Only the enemy stat rows move. So "split the Goburimon tables by difficulty" is answered:
there is nothing to split; the difficulty axis lives entirely in _geneEnemyId.

**Evidence:** scripts/igb_generators.py; docs/systems/drop-tables.md

<a id="f463"></a>
### #463 · unconfirmed

**Where:** 0x16f9fa — NDMW.AFS entry 621 igImage/igClut; #419 pool ordering key; #448 live textures

OFFLINE TEXTURES: igImage._pImage and igClut._pData are content-pool ordering keys (igMemoryRef, the #419 rule) while igImage._pClut is an igObjectRef id that #460 resolves to an igClut record; both buffers are in the pack content pool, so the offline reader needs the #419 pool layout generalised from lists to images/cluts/geometry.

**Notes:** CORRECTION (#464): `_pImage` IS an id -- the global id of its pool block -- not a coincidence. The pool is the blocks concatenated in id order and offset = pool_start + sum of _memSize of MEM ids below it; that is exact on entry 622. The rest of this finding stands.
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
OFFLINE TEXTURE LEAD -- the two texture refs are DIFFERENT KINDS, which is why #447 saw them as one:
 igImage._pImage type 7 = igMemoryRef -> a CONTENT-POOL ordering key (44895 for E_ogre01), exactly
 the kind #419 solved for lists. It is NOT an id: resolving 44895 through the
 remap table lands on a MEM entry only because the number happens to be in range.
 igImage._pClut type 0 = igObjectRef -> a real id, and #460 resolves it: 44715 -> an `igClut`
 record {_fmt 7, _numEntries 256, _stride 4, _clutSize 1024, _pData 45227}, whose
 own `_pData` is again a pool ordering key.
So both buffers (image 4096 B for E_ogre01, clut 1024 B) live in the pack's content pool, laid out by
ascending ordering key. Measured on entry 621: 130 igImage records, `_pImage` keys 167..50041 and
`_imageSize` summing to 270,208 B; the pool is 0x16f9fa..0x4d3862 (3,554,920 B) and holds the images,
the cluts, the geometry and the lists together.

WHAT IS LEFT is exactly the #419 rule GENERALISED: #419 reconstructs the pool from the LIST records
alone and it works because entry 622 has only lists; a texture pack also has igImage / igClut /
geometry memory refs, so the pool reader must enumerate every igMemoryRef field and its block size
(list: count*itemsize; image: _imageSize; clut: _clutSize; geometry: its buffer sizes) and lay them
out in ascending key. Then `_pImage` and `igClut._pData` index straight into the pool with no live
RAM -- the offline equivalent of the live `beTextureCtrlData._tex ->... -> igImage._pImage` route of
#448, whose 46 textures are the ground truth to check against.

**Evidence:** scripts/igb_ids.py; docs/engine/igbinary-format.md

<a id="f464"></a>
### #464 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x0000beec — scripts/igb_pool.py; #419 pool; #460 ids; goal-B offline asset extraction

OFFLINE POOL LAYOUT: an igMemoryRef value is the global id of its pool block, and the content pool is those blocks concatenated in id order (offset = pool_start + sum of _memSize of MEM ids below it). Exact on entry 622 (228 blocks, 7112 bytes; igVec3fList id 0 -> (-135.0,71.92,575.0)); entry 621/179 are short by 141/58 bytes (a few blocks have no id).

**Notes:** REFINEMENT: the 141/58-byte residual is NOT spread through the pool -- it is TRAILING. The last accounted block ends exactly `pool_end - 141` (621) / `- 58` (179), and the bytes there are nonzero and shaped like a float/vec3f stream (`3d 00 00 38 3f 00 00 08 3f...` = 0.719, 0.531,... with a one-byte lead-in), i.e. a NORMAL-buffer style block that no igMemoryDirEntry id accounts for. So block offsets from the prefix sum are CORRECT for the model packs up to that final unaccounted buffer; the earlier 'sane float' fraction of ~0.84 is legitimate variation in lists that hold animation/curve data, not drift. This ties the residual to igVertexArray's `_vdata[1]` normal stream (#458) -- the buffer is in the pool but has no remap id.
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
THE OFFLINE POOL LAYOUT (the key to goal-B asset extraction).

An `igMemoryRef` value is the **global id of its pool block**. The content pool is those blocks
concatenated in id order, so

 offset(block id K) = pool_start + sum(_memSize of every igMemoryDirEntry id < K)

with `pool_start` = the end of the object section and the ids from the remap table (#460). Every
memory ref uses it: a list's `_data`, `igImage._pImage`, `igClut._pData`, geometry buffers.

MEASURED, entry 622 (stag0102's generator pack), exact:
 * 228 pool blocks, their `_memSize` summing to exactly the pool size, 7,112 bytes;
 * igVec3fList id 0 -> pool +0xbeec = floats (-135.0, 71.92, 575.0), a real spawn point -- the same
 kind of value #419 read; igVec3fList id 23 (2 verts) -> (-125.0, 59.96, 295.0), (-105.0,...).

MEASURED, the big model packs, APPROXIMATE:
 * entry 621: 12,362 blocks sum to 3,554,779 vs pool 3,554,920 -- short by 141 bytes;
 * entry 179: short by 58 bytes.
 So a handful of pool blocks in those packs carry no id (or a size that is off), and offsets drift by
 up to that much after the first unaccounted block. A constant shift does not fix it (best sane-float
 fraction ~0.89 at delta ~52), so it is a few local blocks, not a header. This is the one open item
 before offline texture/model extraction is exact for every pack.

CONSEQUENCE: `scripts/igb_pool.py` reads a block by id (`--id`, `--list`, `--check`, `--image NAME
--png out`) and reports the residual, so the port can extract offline today for the generator packs
and needs the residual pinned down for the model packs. `igImage._pImage` IS a block id (correcting
#463's "not an id"), and `igClut._pData` likewise; `igImage._pClut` is an object id -> igClut.
ADDENDUM (measured): the object-pack pool residual is TRAILING, not spread. For
NDMW.AFS entry 621 the pool starts at blob+0x16f9fa and is 3,554,920 bytes; the `_memSize` of
the igMemoryDirEntry ids sums to 3,554,779 and the highest-offset block ends at pool+3,554,779
exactly, so the unaccounted bytes are the LAST 141 (entry 179: 58). Both are therefore a
single trailing region. Note neither is a multiple of 12 (141 = 12*11 + 9, 58 = 12*4 + 10),
so if it is a `vec3f` normal buffer it carries a few extra bytes or the id sizes are short by a
little as well -- which is what makes it worth tying to `igVertexArray._vdata[1]` (finding #458)
before claiming the model packs are exact.

Caveat: the accounting above is only as good as `igb_ids.sections`/`find_table` on a model pack
(the generator packs validate it cell-for-cell, the model packs do not have that check yet).


CORRECTION (finding #478): the rule is `offset = pool_start + sum(align4(_memSize)...)`. Every
block is laid out 4-byte aligned, so a `_memSize` that is not a multiple of 4 is followed by 1-3
padding bytes. The structure above is right; only `align4` was missing, and adding it is what removes
the 'short by N' accounting on the model packs.
 Folded into #478, the current answer for Object-pack content pool layout (4-byte aligned); this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

**Evidence:** scripts/igb_pool.py; docs/engine/igbinary-format.md

<a id="f465"></a>
### #465 · refuted

**Where:** 0x003b641a — scripts/igb_pool.py; #464; live pack = NDMW.AFS 621; live BJGR01.png palette

POOL LAYOUT OPEN FOR THE LARGE PACKS: the per-id _memSize prefix sum is exact for the generator packs but does not locate the model/UI packs' buffers. In entry 621 (identified as the live pack) the live palette of BJGR01.png is absent under raw RGBA, RGBA-RGB-only, CSM1 (LE/BE, both 5->8 expansions, BGR swap) and 4-byte-slot encodings -- best 21/246 colours.

**Notes:** CORRECTED (#467): the palette IS in the file -- in the TEXTURE PACK (entry 625), not in entry 621, which is the stage/object pack and holds only the beTextureCtrlAttr names. The scan below looked in the wrong entry. Texture data is locatable offline; the open problem is the texture pack's own pool offset rule.
------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
SCOPE CORRECTION. The pool block layout is CONFIRMED only for the small/generator
packs; for the LARGE model/UI packs it is NOT verified, and offline texture extraction from them does
not work yet. Evidence, working from NDMW.AFS entry 621 (the live status-screen stage's pack):

 * the pack is identified: its 96 `beTextureCtrlAttr._name` values include ALL 46 texture names the
 live session dumped (BJGR01.png, FD01BJ03.png, stcngmoji.png,...). So 621 is the live pack.
 * the live palette is the ground truth: `BJGR01.png` is a real, coherent 64x64 atlas (gold panels,
 a no-entry sign, marble) and its 246 distinct RGB values are CSM1-expanded ((v<<3)|(v>>2)):
 0,8,16,24,33,41,49,57,66,74,82,90,... -- so the runtime palette is CSM1-derived.
 * THAT PALETTE IS NOT IN THE PACK, under any encoding tried:
 raw RGBA -> best 6/246
 raw RGBA, RGB only -> best 6/246
 CSM1 u16 contiguous LE shift -> best 21/246
 CSM1 u16 contiguous BE shift -> 18/246; 5->8 "*255/31" variants and BGR swaps -> <=14/246
 CSM1 in a 4-byte slot, LE/BE -> 0/246
 (a real palette matches 246/246). Same result for entries 625/626 (the other holders of the name).
 * and it is NOT that the pack's bytes are merely mis-offset: a global set-match scans the WHOLE
 entry, so a wrong offset could not hide it.

So the per-id prefix sum over `_memSize` is exact for the generator packs (622/183/140: sum == pool,
igVec3fList id 0 = a real spawn point) but does not locate a large pack's buffers, and the ~141-byte
total shortfall therefore does not bound the error there. The model/UI pool layout is an OPEN problem.
The live PNGs also have a uniform alpha of 128, which the port's writer may synthesise, so only the RGB
channels were used above. Folded into #467, the current answer for Texture buffers live in texture packs; this finding is kept for its evidence and history.

**Evidence:** scripts/igb_pool.py

<a id="f466"></a>
### #466 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x0010eb38 — beTextureCtrlAttr._name; live_textures.py; offline texture naming for the port

Texture names come from beTextureCtrlAttr._name (the loader assigns igImage._pNameString at runtime): entry 621's 96 beTextureCtrlAttr names include all 46 names the live session dumped, while the igImage._pNameString values are mostly empty.

**Notes:** TEXTURE NAMING IS `beTextureCtrlAttr._name`. A texture's display name is not on the `igImage` in the
pack (most of its `_pNameString` values are empty); the loader copies it from the `beTextureCtrlAttr`
that drives the texture, so at runtime `igImage._pNameString` carries the name. Verified: NDMW.AFS
entry 621 has 96 `beTextureCtrlAttr._name` values and they include ALL 46 names the live session dumped
(BJGR01.png, FD01BJ03.png, FD01BJ05.png,..., stcngmoji.png, stcngwaku01.png, moji.png, num01.png,
bit.png, base01..04.png). So a port can name a texture offline by reading `beTextureCtrlAttr._name`
(or the chain beTextureCtrlAttr._attrSet -> the texture attr) instead of `_pNameString`; the name in
the file already ends with `.png`.

**Evidence:** Running-game texture dump (46 names) compared with NDMW.AFS entry 621's beTextureCtrlAttr._name values

<a id="f467"></a>
### #467 · confirmed

**Where:** 0x001bd0c — NDMW.AFS 625; live_timextures BJGR01.png; scripts/igb_pool.py; corrects #465

Textures live in TEXTURE PACKS (NDMW entry 625/626/618/619...), a separate format: directory + string table + a descriptor table + a flat pool of palettes and index buffers. Entry 621 (the stage pack) holds the beTextureCtrlAttr names but not the buffers. BJGR01.png's palette is at 625+0x1bd0c (4-byte entries FF R G B; 246/246 live match) and its index grid at 625+0x1f911 (found exactly, vertically flipped).

**Notes:** BREAKTHROUGH -- THE TEXTURE BUFFERS ARE IN THE FILE; THEY LIVE IN A *TEXTURE PACK*, NOT THE STAGE PACK.

The earlier "the palette is not in the pack" (#465) was searching the WRONG ENTRY. NDMW.AFS has two
kinds of pack:

 * the STAGE / object pack (entry 621, 5 MB): the igBinary object graph with the two object sections,
 the id remap table and the pool -- and it carries the `beTextureCtrlAttr` records whose `_name` is
 the texture's name. It does NOT hold the texture buffers (its directory has no igImage/igClut data
 of this kind in the pool).
 * the TEXTURE pack (entry 625/626, 199 KB, and 618/619...): its directory lists `igImage`, `igClut`,
 `igTextureAttr`, `beTextureCtrlData`, `igExternalImageEntry`... and after the directory + string
 table it is a **name table + a flat pool** of palettes and index buffers. `decode_record` finds no
 records in it, so it is not the object layout.

WORKED PROOF (entry 625, live session's BJGR01.png):
 * its 256-entry palette is at **+0x1bd0c**. Each entry is 4 bytes `FF R G B` (byte 0 = 0xFF, then
 R,G,B); matching bytes 1..3 against the live PNG gives a **246/246** colour match. (The live
 output has a uniform alpha 0x80 -- the PS2 opaque value -- so the port's writer put that in byte 3
 of the RGBA, not the file's tag byte.)
 * its index buffer (4096 bytes for 64x64 8-bit) is at **+0x1f911**, found EXACTLY: mapping each live
 pixel through that palette to its index and searching the pack finds the grid **vertically
 flipped** at 0x1f911 (gap from the palette 0x3c05).
 * the pack also has a **descriptor table**: for each entry, 8 u32 before the NUL-terminated name
 `{1, 0, 0x27, 8, 0x40, 0, 0, len}` and fields after it that include the palette entry count (0x100)
 and size (0x400) and the dimensions. Byte offsets are NOT stored -- the pool is laid out, so the
 texture pack's offset rule is the thing still to recover.
 * a whole-pack scan shows ~14 palette-like runs (byte offset.. +, e.g. 0x02c0c, 0x0ac0c, 0x0bc0c,
 0x0e90c, 0x1b910, 0x2b908, 0x30114), i.e. the palettes are contiguous blocks in a pool.

So: the offline texture route exists and the buffers are locatable; what remains is the texture pack's
**pool offset rule** (the same *kind* of problem as #464, for a different pack format), plus the
descriptor table's field meanings. Topic head for Texture buffers live in texture packs: also read #465 (folded in, kept for their evidence).

**Evidence:** scripts/igb_pool.py
